therefore gives an invariant covector, as asserted.




                                                       68
Lemma 8.9. Let C be an irreducible component of (p−1 l (S0 ))red . Then C is a smooth curve
of genus one. Its image E = al (C) is an elliptic translate in Al , and al |C : C → E and
pl |C : C → S0 are étale. At every z ∈ C,

                                  Lie(Al )StabGl (z) = Lie(E).

Here Lie(E) means the constant tangent direction of the translate E.

Proof. All components C are projective curves mapping finitely onto S0 . Let J0 be their
generic pointwise stabilizer. More explicitly, discard the proper fixed loci of those finitely
many group elements that do not fix C identically. On the remaining open the stabilizer is
the subgroup J0 fixing C pointwise. Equivariance implies

                                         al (C) ⊂ AJl 0 .

Every component of this fixed locus is a translate of an abelian subvariety with tangent
space VJ0 . Lemma 8.7 bounds its dimension by one, whereas finiteness of al makes the
image of C one-dimensional. It follows that the image is exactly an elliptic translate E, and
VJ0 = Lie(E).
   Write ν : Cb → C for the normalization and let

                                 b : Cb → S0 ,        aC : Cb → E

be the two finite morphisms. At any q ∈ C,   b choose a parameter u on the smooth curve S0
near b(q), and extend it to a local function on B. Such an extension exists because S0 has its
reduced closed-subspace structure. Restricting (8.9) gives

                                         b∗ du = η a∗C θ                                 (8.10)

near q, where θ is a nonzero translation-invariant one-form on E and η is holomorphic. There
is no assumption here that Tl is smooth along C. To justify restriction, first pull (8.9) to
a resolution of Tl . Both sides are regular ordinary differentials there and agree generically,
hence everywhere. A component of the proper base change dominating Cb then gives the
stated equality on C,b since pullback of differentials along a dominant map to a smooth curve
in characteristic zero is injective.
    Equation (8.10) implies pointwise

                                     ramq (b) ≥ ramq (aC ).

Both targets have genus one. Riemann–Hurwitz therefore gives the same total ramification
            b − 2, for both maps. The pointwise inequalities must all be equalities. In
degree, 2g(C)
particular η(q) ̸= 0.
   Put z = ν(q) and J = StabGl (z). The covector in Lemma 8.8 is J-invariant, and the
nonvanishing of η(q) says that its restriction to Lie(E) is nonzero. Since J contains J0 , we
have
                                     VJ ⊂ VJ0 = Lie(E).
A finite-dimensional representation of a finite group in characteristic zero has equally dimen-
sional invariant vector and covector spaces, by the averaging projector. Thus VJ ̸= 0, and the
displayed inclusion is equality. This holds at every point and on every normalization branch.
    Fix a nonzero v0 ∈ Lie(E). Its unique meromorphic lift through al is holomorphic near
each point of C, by Lemma 8.7, and the lifts agree on overlaps by uniqueness. Call the
resulting field ve0 . It has nonzero evaluation everywhere on C, since dal (ve0 ) = v0 . Locally
it descends through pl to a field on B. Its flow preserves the intrinsically defined minimum

                                                 69
stratum S0 , and hence its lifted flow preserves the reduced inverse image and each of its
irreducible curve components. Thus ve0 is tangent to C.
    A reduced curve carrying such a nonvanishing ambient holomorphic field is smooth: choose
a function whose derivative under the field is nonzero, and use the local flow to split off
its disc direction. An invariant reduced curve in this product is the disc times a reduced
zero-dimensional germ. It follows that C = Cb is smooth. Since dal (ve0 ) = v0 ̸= 0, the map
C → E is unramified. It is consequently an étale cover of an elliptic curve, so g(C) = 1. The
equality of ramification divisors already proved also makes C → S0 unramified.

Proof of Proposition 8.1. If a nonquotient germ remained, (8.8) and Lemma 8.9 would supply
C and a nonzero translation direction v0 as above. Choose a positive integer r such that
L = OB (rKB ) is a line bundle, and put

                                            M = p∗l L.

We construct a holomorphic connection on M|C ; in particular we must check both its
extension and its gluing.
   Near z ∈ C, the field ve0 is invariant under the point stabilizer and descends to a
holomorphic field ξz on the corresponding base germ. On Breg , Lie differentiation by ξz acts
on r-fold canonical tensors. It extends to an operator

                                          Dz : L −→ L.

Indeed L is the reflexive extension of its restriction to Breg , so a differentiated section extends
uniquely across the subset of codimension at least two. The operator obeys

                                   Dz (us) = ξz (u)s + uDz (s).

Pull it to the chosen neighborhood in Tl using the lifted symbol ve0 : in a local pulled-back
frame its rule is
                            De z (h p∗ s) = ve0 (h) p∗ s + h p∗ (Dz s).                 (8.11)
                                     l               l        l

The Leibniz identity and ve0 (p∗l u) = p∗l (ξz u) make this definition independent of the frame.
    On an overlap, both operators D   e z have the same symbol. They agree on the dense open
where pl is étale and both varieties are smooth: there the differential of pl identifies the
pulled-back canonical tensors with canonical tensors upstairs, and both operators are Lie
differentiation by the same field ve0 . Their difference is an OTl -linear endomorphism of the
line bundle M; its coefficient is holomorphic and zero on a dense open, hence is zero. Thus
the local operators glue on a neighborhood of C. This argument compares the operators
upstairs and does not require a single descended vector field on a neighborhood of all of S0 .
    Tangency of ve0 to the reduced curve C implies that the glued operator preserves IC M,
by (8.11). It therefore induces a first-order operator

                                       D : M|C −→ M|C

with symbol the nowhere vanishing field ve0 |C . Let τ be the holomorphic one-form on the
elliptic curve C satisfying τ (ve0 ) = 1. Then

                                         ∇s = D(s) ⊗ τ

is a holomorphic connection on M|C .
    A line bundle with a holomorphic connection on a smooth projective curve has degree zero,
a classical connection obstruction [3]. One elementary proof takes a nonzero meromorphic


                                                70
section s: the meromorphic one-form ∇s/s has at each point the residue ordq (s), and the
residue theorem gives deg div(s) = 0. Consequently
                                        deg(M|C ) = 0.
On the other hand, −KB is ample as a Q-Cartier divisor, and pl |C is finite onto the positive-
dimensional curve S0 . Hence
                              deg(M|C ) = r deg (pl |C )∗ KB < 0.
                                                             

This contradiction excludes the nonquotient germ and proves Proposition 8.1.


9     A second Lagrangian system
Retain the local e-locus D and its cohomology marking from Proposition 7.2, and write
f0 : X0 → B for the original projective fibration. The geometric target is an actual nearby
manifold with two integral nef isotropic classes whose entire positive interval lies in its Kähler
cone. This gives both fibrations on one projective manifold, with the first still mapping to
the original B.
Proposition 9.1 (Two systems on one nearby model). Arbitrarily close to the original point
of D, there is a member X ′ = Xt and an integral (1, 1)-class u such that e and u are nef and
                               q(e) = q(u) = 0,       q(e, u) > 0,
and the whole positive interval satisfies
                                  {ae + bu : a, b > 0} ⊂ KX ′ .                              (9.1)
In particular X ′ is projective. Its fixed-base fibration f ′ = ft : X ′ → B admits a second
projective Lagrangian fibration g : X ′ → C, with C normal and projective. For every fiber A
over the smooth locus of g, the restriction f ′ |A : A → B is finite and surjective. Thus B has
a finite cover by an abelian variety.
    The rank-four refinement proved in Proposition 9.6 chooses g with nonzero polarized
variation when the original fibration has that property. We first construct the two classes in
(9.1).
    We distinguish integral isometries from parallel-transport operators throughout the proof.
This distinction matters in applying Torelli. Write Λ = H 2 (X0 , Z), equipped with q, and fix
the connected marked deformation component containing X0 . Its orientation of maximal
positive subspaces is the one given by (Re σ0 , Im σ0 , κ), with κ Kähler. Markings below are
anchored by parallel transport in this component.

9.1   Realized positive three-planes
Call an oriented positive three-plane T ⊂ ΛR realized if it is the span of the period plane and
a Kähler class of a marked manifold in the chosen component. The Calabi–Yau theorem
and twistor rotation imply that every compatible oriented two-plane P ⊂ T , together with
its positively oriented orthogonal ray in T , is realized once T is realized. We use period
surjectivity from the chosen marked component for the integral Beauville lattice [24, §3.1 and
Theorem 5.5], together with the marked period identification [24, Corollaries 4.10 and 5.9].
The Kähler form of parallel-transport Torelli says that a Hodge parallel-transport isometry
carrying a Kähler class to a Kähler class is induced by an isomorphism [33, Theorem 1.3(2)
and §3.2]. The global Torelli input in that proof is the preceding marked period identification.
These are the marked-component statements used below. The twistor description is also
recalled in [2, Section 5.1].

                                               71
Lemma 9.2. Realized positive three-planes form an open subset. In rank greater than four,
a three-plane fails to be realized precisely when it is orthogonal to a rational negative MBM
class of the marked component. In rank four, the weaker assertion that every nonrealized
three-plane has a rational negative orthogonal line suffices and holds. At a fixed oriented
period, Kähler cones of marked models in the component are either equal or disjoint.

Proof. Given a realized pair (P, κ), nearby planes T ′ contain oriented period planes P ′ close
to P and orthogonal positive vectors κ′ close to κ. Local Torelli realizes P ′ by a small
deformation of that manifold. Openness of the relative Kähler cone makes κ′ Kähler, proving
openness.
    Recall the two parts of the MBM theorem used here: the negative rational wall classes
are invariant under deformation that keeps them of type (1, 1), and the Kähler cone is a
component of the positive cone after removing their orthogonal hyperplanes [2, Theorems 1.17
and 1.19]. No bound on their squares is used. A realized plane cannot be orthogonal to
such a class: that class would be of type (1, 1) and orthogonal to a Kähler class. Conversely,
suppose T has no such orthogonality. Choose a generic oriented plane P ⊂ T , so that every
rational class orthogonal to P is already orthogonal to T . Indeed, each rational vector not
orthogonal to T excludes a proper closed subset of the sphere of planes in T , and there are
only countably many such vectors. Period surjectivity gives a marked manifold with period
P . Its rational (1, 1) classes include no MBM class. The cone theorem therefore identifies its
Kähler cone with the whole positive component. The orthogonal ray to P in T , with sign
fixed by the orientation, is in that component. This realizes T .
    For rank four one can avoid even the MBM formulation in this step. If the negative line
  ⊥
T is irrational, the same generic choice of P has zero Picard lattice. Its Kähler cone is the
whole positive component, so T is realized. Thus a nonrealized plane has rational negative
normal line.
    Finally, an intersection between the Kähler cones of two marked models with the same
period gives a Hodge parallel-transport isometry carrying a Kähler class to a Kähler class.
Torelli makes it the isometry of an isomorphism. The cones then agree.

    Choose an integral ample class h on X0 . A first arbitrarily small deformation preserving
both h and e makes the real period plane P∗ rational: rational positive two-planes are dense
in the rational space ⟨e, h⟩⊥ . In rank four this plane is already rational, and we take the
original point. Ampleness of h persists, and Proposition 7.2 preserves the fibration of class e.
Denote this projective member and its fibration by X∗ , f∗ . Set

                          U = P∗ ⊕ ⟨e, h⟩Q ,        sign(q|U ) = (3, 1).                  (9.2)

Here and below a rational plane is also used for its real span when discussing signs and
periods. The space P∗ + Rh is realized. Consequently U ⊥ contains no MBM class that could
obstruct realization.
    Let H(U ) be the hyperbolic three-space of negative lines in UR . A point [v] corresponds
to the positive three-plane v ⊥ ∩ UR , with its fixed orientation. Let J ⊂ H(U ) be the set of
nonrealized planes. It is closed by Lemma 9.2. Moreover,

                                        J ⊂ H(U )(Q).                                     (9.3)

For rank greater than four, project an obstructing rational MBM class orthogonally to U .
The projection is rational and nonzero, since U ⊥ contains no such class; it is the negative
normal line in U . In rank four use the last assertion about nonrealized planes in Lemma 9.2.
Any parallel-transport isometry preserving U preserves J.



                                               72
    Choose the component of the negative cone whose null boundary contains the ray R>0 e.
We call its nonzero vectors, including boundary vectors, future vectors. Distinct future null
vectors have negative pairing. The other null line in ⟨e, h⟩ has a rational future representative

                                                    2q(e, h)
                                      w∗ = e −               h.                            (9.4)
                                                     q(h)

The negative lines in a Lorentz plane with future null boundary vectors v, w form the geodesic
{[sv + tw] : s, t > 0}; we denote it by (v, w).

9.2   Null rotations from actual algebraic families
Lemma 9.3. Let a ∈ UQ be nonzero and null. Every rational null rotation fixing a, extended
as the identity on U ⊥ , has a positive power that is an integral parallel-transport operator in
the chosen marked component.

Proof. Choose b ∈ UQ with q(a, b) = 1 and q(b) = 0; subtracting q(b0 )a/2 from any b0 paired
to 1 with a gives such a vector. Let V = ⟨a, b⟩⊥ ∩ U , a positive rational plane. For x ∈ VQ
the null rotation is
                      Ta,x a = a,
                       Ta,x b = b + x − 21 q(x)a,                 Ta,x |U ⊥ = id .         (9.5)
                       Ta,x v = v − q(v, x)a   (v ∈ V ),

Direct pairing verifies that it preserves q, and substitution gives Ta,x Ta,y = Ta,x+y . Thus
  m =T
Ta,x     a,mx . Its matrix entries in an integral basis are polynomials in m of degree at most
two with rational coefficients and constant term the identity. For sufficiently divisible m,
both Ta,mx and its inverse are integral. This accounts also for the finite gluing between the
sublattices in U and U ⊥ .
    For x ̸= 0, choose a rational positive vector ℓ ∈ V ∩ x⊥ . The rotation fixes ℓ. Replace it
by a primitive integral vector on the same line. A generic period in ℓ⊥ has Picard lattice Zℓ.
Period surjectivity and the cone theorem give an ample marked deformation with polarization
ℓ or −ℓ; choose the ample sign. This sign does not alter the stabilizer of ℓ.
    We now use the prescribed-polarization conclusion of [44, Lemma 4.2]. It applies to this
primitive positive class ℓ, ample on a manifold in the chosen marked component. Write Dℓ
for the component of its type-IV period domain containing that ample period, and put

                         Gℓ (Z) = {g ∈ O(Λ, q) : gℓ = ℓ, gDℓ = Dℓ }.

The cited lemma supplies a smooth projective family of IHS manifolds over a smooth connected
quasi-projective variety S, with a relative polarization of class kℓ for some k > 0 and a
reference marking obtained by parallel transport from X0 . It also supplies a torsion-free
finite-index subgroup Γ ⊂ Gℓ (Z) containing the family monodromy and an algebraic period
map
                                  Φ : S −→ Qℓ := Γ\Dℓ .
Here Qℓ is smooth and quasi-projective, the map Dℓ → Qℓ is a covering with free Γ-action,
and a local marked lift of Φ is submersive. The type-IV domain Dℓ is contractible, including
in rank four, so this covering identifies π1 (Qℓ ) with Γ.
    The remaining finite-index assertion concerns the image of this actual family monodromy,
and we prove it here. The submersive local lift makes the algebraic map Φ dominant.
    Choose an irreducible algebraic slice S ′ ⊂ S of dimension dim Qℓ dominating Qℓ . For
example, intersect with general hyperplanes through a smooth submersive point, transverse

                                                73
to its period fiber. After normalization and restriction, this slice is smooth and generically
finite over Qℓ . There is a nonempty Zariski-open Q◦ ⊂ Qℓ over which its restriction is a
nonempty finite étale cover: generic finiteness gives a finite open after removing the boundary
in a proper compactification, and characteristic zero allows removal of the branch locus.
Therefore its fundamental-group image has finite index in π1 (Q◦ ). The map π1 (Q◦ ) → π1 (Qℓ )
is surjective, since a loop in a complex manifold can be moved off a proper algebraic subset.
It follows that
                                      [Γ : Φ∗ π1 (S)] < ∞.                                 (9.6)
The period map on the universal cover of S, based at this reference marking, is equivariant
for the actual second-cohomology monodromy. Because Dℓ → Qℓ is a covering with free
action, the deck transformation induced by each loop is exactly that cohomology operator.
Hence the subgroup in (9.6) consists of actual parallel transports, with the original marking
retained.
    Our rotation fixes ℓ; after taking an integral power, a further power preserves Dℓ and
therefore lies in Gℓ (Z). Since Γ has finite index in Gℓ (Z), a further power lies in Γ; by (9.6),
one more positive power lies in the family monodromy. This uses only that every element
has a positive power in any finite-index subgroup; normality of the subgroup is unnecessary.
Transport to the original reference fiber finishes the proof. The zero parameter gives the
identity.

9.3    An integral loxodromic operator with irrational axis
Lemma 9.4. There is a parallel-transport operator γ preserving U and acting identically on
U ⊥ such that its characteristic polynomial on U is irreducible over Q. Its real eigenvalues
are ρ, ρ−1 with ρ > 1, and its other two eigenvalues are a nonreal conjugate pair on the unit
circle. Its attracting and repelling future null lines a+ , a− can both be placed in any prescribed
neighborhood of [w∗ ] in the null boundary.

Proof. Choose a rational null partner b of e on the line of w∗ with q(e, b) = 1, and a
rational orthogonal basis v1 , v2 of their positive perpendicular plane. Write q(v1 ) = A > 0,

                                        2(A − Bt2 )

opposite rotations Te,m1 x and Tb,m2 y are integral monodromies.

    The characteristic polynomial on U is monic with rational coefficients, and its roots are
algebraic integers because γk preserves Λ. It therefore belongs to Z[z]. It is reciprocal, has
constant term 1, and has no rational root: a rational root would be 1 or −1. If it factored
into two quadratics over Q, real coefficients would force the nonreal pair to belong to one
quadratic. That monic factor has integral coefficients, so the sum of the pair would be an
integer, a contradiction. Thus it is irreducible. Fix a sufficiently large k.
    Finally, a nontrivial null rotation fixing [w∗ ] has powers converging to [w∗ ] on every other
point of the null boundary. In boundary coordinates with [w∗ ] at infinity these powers are
translations z 7→ z + jc, with c ̸= 0. Such a rotation has an integral monodromy power by
Lemma 9.3. Neither eigenline of γk is rational, by irreducibility, so neither is [w∗ ]. Conjugating
γk by a sufficiently high power of this rotation brings both eigenlines as close to [w∗ ] as
desired and preserves all the asserted properties. Call the conjugate γ.

    The axis (a− , a+ ) contains no rational negative line. Indeed, a nonzero rational vector
in its invariant real Lorentz plane would have its entire γ-orbit in that plane. The rational
span of the orbit would be a nonzero proper rational invariant subspace of U , contradicting
irreducibility. The two geodesics (e, a± ) also contain no rational negative line. Otherwise
such a line, together with rational e, would define a rational Lorentz plane. A rational binary
quadratic form with one rational null line has both null lines rational: if q(e, v) ̸= 0, its second
null vector is v − q(v)e/(2q(e, v)). The second line would be a± , contrary to irreducibility.
Equation (9.3) gives

                         J ∩ (a− , a+ ) = J ∩ (e, a+ ) = J ∩ (e, a− ) = ∅.                    (9.9)

9.4    Uniform tails and the entire Kähler interval
For a future null line [w] near [w∗ ], choose its representative with q(e, w) = −1 and put

                                     P (e, w) = ⟨e, w⟩⊥ ∩ UR ,

oriented near P∗ . This is a nearby e-period. It gives an actual small deformation Xw of X∗
within D, with the fibration fw : Xw → B by Proposition 7.2. The orthogonal projection of
h to ⟨e, w⟩ has the form

               hw = α(w)e − βw,         α(w) = −q(h, w) > 0,        β = q(h, e) > 0.         (9.10)

It tends to h as [w] → [w∗ ], so it is Kähler on Xw after restricting to a small neighborhood.
As e is nef on Xw , every hw + te, t ≥ 0, is Kähler. Its orthogonal negative line in ⟨e, w⟩ is
[(α(w) + t)e + βw]. On a smaller neighborhood V of [w∗ ], the continuous function α(w)/β is
bounded above by some R. Thus

                       [se + w] ∈
                                /J       ([w] ∈ V, q(e, w) = −1, s ≥ R).                     (9.11)

This is the required uniformity near the rational endpoint e. Choose the conjugation in
Lemma 9.4 so that both a+ and a− belong to V.

Lemma 9.5. For all sufficiently large integers N ,

                                        (e, γ N e) ∩ J = ∅.

Proof. Choose future eigenvectors for a+ , a− with eigenvalues ρ, ρ−1 , respectively. The
remaining real invariant plane is positive definite and γ acts on it by a rotation. Since e is a
future null vector different from both eigenlines, its coefficients along both future eigenvectors
are positive. Indeed, pairing its eigenspace decomposition with either eigenvector shows the
coefficient of the other to be positive. It follows that

                           ρ−N γ N e −→ v+ ,         ρ−N γ −N e −→ v− ,                     (9.12)

where v+ , v− are positive representatives of a+ , a− .
    If the assertion failed, choose N → ∞ and zN = [sN e + γ N e] ∈ J, with sN > 0. We first

                                sN ρ−N −→ 0,        sN ρN −→ ∞.                      (9.13)

positive constant, and [γ N e] → a+ ∈ V. The tail exclusion (9.11) bounds sN /cN above. If a
subsequence of sN /ρN had a positive limit, zN would converge to an interior point of (e, a+ ).
Closedness of J would put that point in J, contradicting (9.9). This proves the first limit.

                                      1 ≤ sN ρ2kN −N < ρ2 .

The limit has negative square, so its line is an interior point of the axis (a− , a+ ). Invariance

    Take such an N and let XN be the actual nearby e-deformation with first fibration
fN : XN → B and period PN = P (e, γ N e), oriented near P∗ . The choice of a+ close to [w∗ ],
followed by large N , makes this deformation as small as prescribed. Put

                         uN = −γ N e,       CN = {ae + buN : a, b > 0}.                     (9.14)

The minus sign is essential: e and γ N e are future null vectors, so q(e, γ N e) < 0 and
q(e, uN ) > 0. The cone CN is a positive component in the Lorentz plane ⟨e, uN ⟩. Its positive
rays correspond, by taking orthogonal negative lines, to the geodesic (e, γ N e). Lemma 9.5
and twistor rotation show that every ray of CN is Kähler on some marked model of period
PN .
    All those rays are Kähler on XN itself. Indeed, their projectivized interval is connected.
Intersections with the Kähler cones of marked models give an open cover by pairwise disjoint


                                                76
sets, by Lemma 9.2. Such a cover has just one nonempty member. The class hw of (9.10),
for [w] = [γ N e], belongs to this interval and is Kähler on XN . Thus

                                           CN ⊂ KXN .                                      (9.15)

In particular e + uN is an integral Kähler class, making XN projective, and uN is a nonzero
integral nef isotropic (1, 1) class on this very manifold. It is the Chern class of a line bundle:
the exponential sequence and H 1 (XN , OXN ) = 0 identify Pic(XN ) with the integral (1, 1)
classes. Applying Theorem 2.4, including Stein factorization, gives the second Lagrangian
fibration g : XN → C with normal projective base C. Both fN and g are projective morphisms:
their graphs are closed analytic subvarieties of projective products, hence algebraic, and the
corresponding algebraic maps are projective.

9.5   Finite maps from its abelian fibers
Let A be a fiber over the smooth locus of g. It is an abelian variety after choosing an origin.
A positive multiple of uN is g ∗ c1 (HC ) for an ample line bundle HC on C, so the cohomology
class of A is a positive rational multiple of unN . To see the constant, cut C by n general
hyperplanes in a sufficiently high multiple of HC ; their transverse intersection is a finite
collection of regular points, and its inverse image is the corresponding collection of fibers.
All regular fibers have the same cohomology class by parallel transport over the connected
smooth locus.
    Polarizing Fujiki gives the exact positive quantity
                                  !Z
                             2n
                                        en unN = cXN 2n q(e, uN )n > 0.                    (9.16)
                             n     XN

Therefore the nef line bundle fN∗ H| has positive top self-intersection. Such a line bundle
                                    A
on an abelian variety is ample. Indeed, average representatives of its class by translations.
Nefness makes the resulting invariant Hermitian form positive semidefinite, and positive top
intersection makes its determinant positive. All its eigenvalues are then positive, so the line
bundle has a positive curvature representative and is ample.
    The morphism fN |A has no positive-dimensional fiber, since a curve in such a fiber would
have degree zero against this ample line bundle. It is proper, hence finite. Its image is
closed of dimension dim A = n = dim B and therefore equals B. This proves all assertions of
Proposition 9.1. In particular we have obtained

                                           p : A −→ B                                      (9.17)

as a finite surjective morphism from an abelian variety.

9.6   Nonzero variation in rank four
Proposition 9.6 (The rank-four variation refinement). If b2 (X0 ) = 4 and the polarized
family of smooth fibers of f0 has nonzero variation, the choices in Proposition 9.1 can be
made so that g also has nonzero polarized variation.

Proof. Nonzero variation means that, at some regular fiber, the derivative in a base direction of
its weight-one period map is nonzero. This condition persists under small deformations of the
fibration: on a small smooth product neighborhood in the family supplied by Proposition 7.2,
the relative Hodge bundle and that derivative vary holomorphically, so a chosen nonzero
matrix entry stays nonzero.


                                                77
    The fiber polarization can also be transported. Its class is the restriction of an integral
ample class on X0 . By Lemma 7.1 and topological transport, the restriction image on a
nearby smooth fiber is a rational one-dimensional space with kernel e⊥ . The restriction of a
Kähler class spans its real form, since it pairs positively with e, so the transported integral
class remains of type (1, 1). Positivity on the smooth torus is open. This gives a polarized
family near the chosen fiber, and the same argument applies at the conjugate fibration.
    In rank four the preliminary point was X∗ = X0 . Complex conjugation of its hyperkähler
complex structure reverses the period-plane orientation and the Kähler sign. The resulting
marked manifold X ∗ has period P ∗ , ample class −h, and conjugate fibration f ∗ : X ∗ → B
with fiber class −e. It lies in the same marked deformation component: a half-circle of
the twistor sphere joins the complex structure to its negative. Nonzero variation of f ∗ is
equivalent to that of f∗ .
    We choose the endpoint neighborhood in Lemma 9.4 small enough for the following
comparison with this conjugate fibration. Under the actual parallel transport γ −N , the
underlying plane of PN becomes
                                       ⟨γ −N e, e⟩⊥ ∩ UR .
It approaches an unoriented plane near P∗ , since [γ −N e] → a− and a− can be chosen
arbitrarily close to [w∗ ]. The transported positive cone is

                             γ −N CN = {aγ −N e − be : a, b > 0}.                        (9.18)

By (9.4), this cone contains a vector close to −h. Preservation of the orientation of maximal
positive three-spaces therefore makes the transported oriented period close to P ∗ .
    Choose the endpoint neighborhood sufficiently small, and then N sufficiently large, that
this period lies in a neighborhood where the (−e)-deformation of f ∗ exists and has nonzero
variation. Let YN be that actual small deformation of X ∗ with period γ −N PN . It has a
Kähler class near −h, in the component (9.18). In rank four, H 1,1 (XN , R) = ⟨e, uN ⟩R , so
(9.15) says that the entire positive component of XN is Kähler. The Hodge parallel-transport
isometry between YN and XN induced by γ N consequently carries a Kähler class to a Kähler
class. Torelli identifies these manifolds by an isomorphism inducing that isometry. The class
−e on YN becomes uN on XN .
    Line bundles on an IHS manifold are determined by their Chern classes. Thus the trans-
ported fibration and the fibration obtained from uN have the same connected fibers: positive
generated powers define the same Stein factorization. It follows that g has nonzero polarized
variation. The isometry in this comparison is the actual parallel transport constructed in
Lemma 9.3.


10     The varying case when b2 = 4
Proposition 10.1 (The varying rank-four case). Let X be a projective irreducible holomorphic
symplectic manifold of dimension 2n with b2 (X) = 4, and let

                                  f : X −→ B,         g : X −→ C

be projective Lagrangian fibrations with normal projective bases. Assume that the polarized
family of smooth fibers of g has nonzero variation and that, for every smooth fiber At of
g, the restriction f |At : At → B is finite and surjective. Fix a connected minimum stratum
S0 ⊂ B and its smooth Deligne–Mumford stack S as in Propositions 4.3 and 4.5. Then
                                                                 X
                  H a,b (S) = 0    for a ̸= b,    χtop (S0 ) =       hp,p (S) > 0.
                                                                 p


                                                 78
    Proposition 9.6 supplies these two fibrations when the original rank-four fibration has
nonzero polarized variation, with the first fibration still mapping to the original base B. The
positive Euler characteristic in the proposition contradicts Proposition 6.1 when the fixed
stratum has a singular maximal chart.
    Fix the data in the proposition, and let C ◦ be the connected smooth open of regular
values of g. Resolve the reduced inverse image of S0 in g −1 (C ◦ ), retaining the components
which dominate C ◦ , and restrict to a connected dense algebraic open T ⊂ C ◦ over which
the resolution is smooth. The base T is smooth and quasi-projective. We obtain smooth
projective families
                           π : A = g −1 (T ) −→ T,       ρ : Q −→ T,
with maps j : Q → A and h : Q → S. The latter map uses the normalized maximal-chart
covers, which are étale over the smooth source by purity; it therefore retains all the stabilizer
data of S. Put d = dim S0 . Each Qt has dimension d, possibly with several components, and
its map to S0 is dominant and generically finite. Indeed At → B is finite. The proper closed
images of the omitted nondominating components can be removed from T . Put

                        VQ = R1 π∗ Q,      UQi = Ri ρ∗ Q,          H1 = V 1,0 .

An origin on the fibers At is unnecessary: their holomorphic one-forms are translation
invariant and trivialize their cotangent bundles. All variations in this section are taken over
the same algebraic open T .
    To prove diagonal cohomology, suppose that 0 ̸= α ∈ H a,b (S) with a > b. We will turn its
pullbacks into a flat tensor whose exterior factor has a nonzero all-holomorphic component.
We will find a joint monodromy lowering operator whose block from V 1,0 to V 0,1 is invertible,
whereas its action on the factor U b vanishes after b + 1 lowerings. Invariance of the tensor will
then force its ath exterior lowering to vanish, contradicting the all-holomorphic component.
The finite maps At → B produce the tensor; the rank-four invariant-cycle count, together
with the nonzero variation of g, produces the invertible lowering operator.
Lemma 10.2 (Differentials and the top cup component). For every t ∈ T there is a natural
factorization
                              h∗t Ω1S −→ jt∗ Ω1At −→ Ω1Qt .                       (10.1)
If 0 ̸= α ∈ H a,b (S), its pullback is a nonzero global flat section y of UCa+b . Moreover, for the
morphism of variations

                      T : Λa VQ ⊗ UQb −→ UQa+b ,        v ⊗ u 7−→ j ∗ v ⌣ u,                (10.2)

we have, at every point of T ,

                                   yt ∈ Tt Λa H1,t ⊗ (Utb )0,b .
                                                              
                                                                                            (10.3)

Proof. We first check the independence needed to define (10.1). On a maximal chart, lift
a form on the smooth orbit to an ordinary ambient differential, using the local product
presentation of Proposition 4.5. Every tangent vector to the smooth finite cover of this chart
maps, at a point above the orbit, into the orbit tangent space. Here is the trace argument in
this finite-map situation. Work with a pointed finite local branch R ⊂ E of generic degree m,
with E regular. Extend a tangent vector upstairs to a local derivation δ of E. The rule

                                 r 7−→ m−1 TrFrac(E)/ Frac(R) (δr)

is a derivation of R: its values are regular by normality, and the Leibniz rule follows from
R-linearity of trace. Its value at the marked point is the differential image of the chosen

                                                79
tangent vector, since all conjugates in the pointed branch have the same residue and trace on
the residue is multiplication by m. Evaluations of ambient derivations are precisely the orbit
tangent directions, by Proposition 4.3.
    The normalized pullback of a maximal chart to At is smooth and étale over At . Indeed
the quasi-étale exceptional locus has codimension at least two, as does its inverse image
under the finite map At → B, and purity applies. Thus the preceding tangent calculation
applies there. The difference of two lifts of an orbit form vanishes on the orbit tangent, so its
pullback vanishes as an ambient cotangent vector at every point above the orbit. Restricting
to the reduced inverse image and then pulling to its resolution kills this difference. The
resulting maps are compatible on the chart overlaps and with the finite group actions; hence
they descend to (10.1).
    The map of the entire family to the fixed stack S makes h∗ α a global flat section. It is
nonzero on every fiber. To see this, choose β ∈ H d−a,d−b (S) with α ⌣ β ̸= 0, using Poincaré
duality and the Hodge decomposition on S. Pullback on H 2d (S, C) is nonzero: the dth power
of a coarse ample class has positive integral on a dominating component of Qt . This also
holds for d = 0, where the class is the unit. Since S is connected, its top cohomology is
one-dimensional; consequently h∗t (α ⌣ β) ̸= 0 and h∗t α ̸= 0.
    Finally, take the ath exterior power of (10.1), and use

                                jt∗ ΩaAt = Λa H 0 (At , Ω1At ) ⊗ OQt .

On H b the resulting sheaf maps factor Dolbeault pullback through

                                Λa H 0 (At , Ω1At ) ⊗ H b (Qt , OQt ).

Under the Dolbeault identification the last arrow is cup product with the pulled-back
holomorphic forms. This is exactly (10.3).

   Choose flat polarizations on the variations in (10.2), and the induced tensor polarization
on its source. Write T † for the adjoint. This adjoint is flat, although the Hodge metrics
themselves generally vary. Indeed, both sides of (10.2) have weight a + b, and a morphism of
Hodge structures commutes with their Weil operators. The adjoint for their positive Hodge
metrics is therefore its transpose for the flat polarization forms. It is a morphism of variations.
Consequently
                                  z = T † y ∈ Γ(T, Λa VC ⊗ UCb )                            (10.4)
is flat. Its component
                                     ztop ∈ Λa H1 ⊗ (U b )0,b                               (10.5)
is nonzero at every point. In fact, choose w in this component with T w = y. If the orthogonal
projection of z to this component were zero, then

                               0 = ⟨z, w⟩ = ⟨y, T w⟩ = ⟨y, y⟩ > 0,

a contradiction. Orthogonality here follows from the factor Hodge decompositions of the
tensor Hodge metric.

Lemma 10.3 (The simultaneous Higgs operator). Let T be a connected smooth quasi-
projective algebraic variety, and let π : A → T and ρ : Q → T be smooth projective
morphisms. The fibers of ρ may be disconnected. For an integer b ≥ 0, put VQ = R1 π∗ Q and
UQb = Rb ρ∗ Q, choose flat polarizations on these rational variations, and write H1 = V 1,0 .
Assume the following fixed-part condition for every pure tensor word in VQ , UQb and their
duals: its full-monodromy invariant subspace is a constant Hodge structure, so every Hodge


                                                 80
component of a global flat invariant tensor is again globally flat. This condition holds for the
geometric systems just specified, as verified below.
    Let G be the full Zariski closure of the monodromy on VQ ⊕ UQb . Then G is linearly
reductive, though it may be disconnected. Its Lie algebra gC , transported by conjugation as a
flat subbundle of End(VC ) ⊕ End(UCb ), is a subvariation of Hodge structure of weight zero.
For every tangent vector x ∈ Tt T ,

                                      (θV,x , θU,x ) ∈ g−1,1
                                                        t    .                            (10.6)

The image
                      Et = im g−1,1                               ∗
                                    −→ Hom(H1,t , H 1,t ) ⊂ Sym2 H1,t
                                                                 
                               t                                                          (10.7)
is a subbundle preserved by the Chern connection. In unitary frames it satisfies

                          AB ∗ C + CB ∗ A ∈ Et          (A, B, C ∈ Et ),                  (10.8)

where B ∗ is the Hermitian adjoint matrix.

Proof. First we verify the stated fixed-part condition from the geometric hypotheses. Write
U = U b . A pure tensor word has the form
                                                  ′                      ′
                            M = V ⊗r ⊗ (V ∨ )⊗r ⊗ U ⊗s ⊗ (U ∨ )⊗s .

The polarizations give V ∨ ≃ V (1) and U ∨ ≃ U (b), so
                                           ′             ′
                             M ≃ V ⊗(r+r ) ⊗ U ⊗(s+s ) (r′ + bs′ ).
                                                             

                                                                     ′       ′
The untwisted word is a flat Hodge direct summand of Rr+r +b(s+s ) ϖ∗ Q for the corresponding
fiber product ϖ : Y → T of copies of π and ρ. The canonical Künneth inclusion and projection
commute with monodromy and Hodge decomposition; no algebraic Künneth correspondence
is required. The product remains smooth and projective when fibers of ρ are disconnected.
Deligne’s Theorem 4.1.1(ii) and Corollary 4.1.2 of [16] supply invariant lifts from a nonsingular
compactification of this product and make their Hodge components globally flat. Applying
the Künneth projection and then the Tate twist proves the fixed-part condition for M. It
also proves it for flat Hodge direct summands such as exterior and symmetric powers. The
summand projectors decompose every tensor construction on V ⊕ U into these pure words.
Since T is connected, global flat sections are exactly full-monodromy invariants, and taking
the Zariski closure does not change them.
    The same geometric hypotheses give semisimplicity of VQ ⊕ UQb by Deligne’s Proposi-
tion 4.2.5 and Theorem 4.2.6 of [16]. Both cohomology systems have rational structures,
integral lattices modulo torsion, and polarizations on the smooth algebraic base T . Semisim-
plicity and faithfulness imply that the unipotent radical of G◦ is trivial. The component
group G/G◦ is finite, so averaging complements in characteristic zero makes the full, possibly
disconnected group G linearly reductive.
    A linearly reductive subgroup of a general linear group is the pointwise stabilizer of its
invariant mixed tensors. One way to check the pointwise assertion is to realize the subgroup
as the stabilizer of a line in a rational representation, realize that representation in mixed
tensors, and choose an invariant complementary subspace to the line by linear reductivity. Its
projection is an invariant tensor; fixing that tensor preserves the line and hence belongs to the
subgroup. Applying this to the faithful representation V ⊕ U b , with its invariant summand
projectors included, gives

                 gt = {D : Dτ = 0 for every invariant mixed tensor τ at t}.               (10.9)

                                                81
    For a flat invariant tensor of a single Hodge type, the three parts of its connection have
different Hodge degrees. Its lowering part therefore vanishes separately. Applying this to every
flat Hodge component just described shows that the simultaneous pair in (10.6) annihilates
every tensor in (10.9). This proves (10.6); it is an assertion about the Lie algebra of the
pointwise stabilizer. Also, the invariant tensor spaces are Hodge graded, so their common
infinitesimal annihilator is Hodge graded. It is flat by conjugation transport, and therefore is
a subvariation of the indicated weight-zero endomorphism variation.
    The grading-preserving part of its connection is the restriction of the ambient Chern
connection. Projection to End(V ) is a morphism of variations; its image is a subvariation,
and its degree (−1, 1) part is E. This proves constant rank and Chern parallelism. The
weight-one polarization identifies H 1 with H1∗ and makes these lowering matrices symmetric.
    For completeness, the bracket calculation takes place in the polarization-preserving Lie
algebra. In a unitary Hodge frame a lowering operator has block matrix
                                                !
                                           0 0
                                LA =                  on H1 ⊕ H 1 .
                                           A 0

The conjugate of a lift of LB in g−1,1 is in g1,−1 and has upper block B ∗ , up to the same
irrelevant polarization sign for all B. The double bracket of lifts of LA , this upper operator,
and LC has lower block AB ∗ C + CB ∗ A, up to sign. Its Hodge degree is (−1, 1); projection
to the first factor proves (10.8).

   Apply the lemma to the geometric systems constructed above; its fixed-part condition
was verified in the proof. The Higgs matrices in E have an additional symmetry supplied by
the Lagrangian system. Symplectic contraction gives an isomorphism
                                       ∼
                              ξ : TT −−→ H1 ,         x 7−→ [ιxeσ|At ],                 (10.10)

independent of the lift xe since the fiber is Lagrangian. Regard ξ as a VC -valued one-form. It
satisfies d∇ ξ = 0. Indeed its pairing with any locally transported fiber one-cycle is the fiber
integral of the closed form σ; exterior differentiation commutes with this integration, and the
restriction of σ to the fibers is zero. Taking the V 0,1 component gives

                                   θV,x (ξ(y)) = θV,y (ξ(x)).

The polarization already gives symmetry in the two matrix slots. After the identification
(10.10), these two symmetries give a cubic
                                  ∗
                       ct ∈ Sym3 H1,t ,      ct (x, −, −) = θV,ξ−1 (x) ∈ Et .           (10.11)

This is the Donagi–Markman cubic associated with a Lagrangian system [18, Section 7.2,
Lemmas 7.1–7.2 and Remark 7.3]; the calculation above gives the symmetry in the conventions
used here. Nonzero variation means that this cubic is not identically zero.

Lemma 10.4 (Maximal-rank kernels and a symmetric cubic). Let H be a finite-dimensional
Hermitian vector space and let E ⊂ Sym2 H ∗ be a complex subspace satisfying (10.8) in
unitary coordinates. Suppose c ∈ Sym3 H ∗ and c(x, −, −) ∈ E for every x ∈ H. If A ∈ E has

                                c(k, −, −) = 0         (k ∈ ker A).

inside E. A nonzero KK block would, when added to P , have rank larger than r. Hence
every member of E has zero KK block. Write

Maximality of r implies B t B = 0. Polarizing in B shows that, for a fixed k ∈ K, the space

Lemma 10.5 (Existence of an invertible lowering matrix). Under the hypotheses of Proposi-
tion 10.1, every fiber of the bundle E attached to the families π, ρ above contains an invertible
matrix.




                                                   83
Proof. Let r be the maximum rank in Et . It is independent of t, because Chern parallel

                                  Ktall =            ker A ⊂ H1,t .                      (10.15)
                                             A∈Et

Unitary Chern transport preserves the entire collection of these kernels, and therefore their
span. Thus (10.15) is a global Chern-parallel subbundle of constant rank. No matrix P ,
chosen kernel, or finite cover enters its definition.
   By Lemma 10.4, the cubic vanishes when any argument lies in K all . In particular all
lowering Higgs operators annihilate this subbundle. Write the real flat connection on V as

                                        ∇ = D + θ + θ,

with D the Chern connection. On K all ⊂ V 1,0 , θ vanishes by the cubic and θ vanishes by
Hodge degree. Complex conjugation in the real variation shows that θ vanishes on K all ⊂ V 0,1 ,
while θ vanishes there by degree. Consequently

                                            K all ⊕ K all

is the complexification of a real flat subbundle L ⊂ VR ; its flat connection is unitary.
Positivity of the Hodge metric shows that the alternating polarization is nondegenerate on L.
Its symplectic orthogonal L⊥ is also a global flat subbundle. These statements concern the
full flat connection on T , and hence full monodromy around every loop.
    We now compute the relevant full-monodromy invariant space. The use of the one-
dimensional fiber-restriction image to prove irreducibility over R also appears in Bakker’s
proof of Matsushita’s variation conjecture [4, Lemma 5], building on Voisin’s argument. Here
it rules out the specific flat summand extracted from the maximal-rank kernels. By the global
invariant-cycle theorem, in the form of [16, Theorem 4.1.1(ii)] applied to g −1 (C ◦ ) → C ◦ with
smooth projective compactification X, the image of

                              H 2 (X, Q) −→ H 2 (At , Q) = Λ2 VQ,t

is precisely the invariant subspace. Its dimension is one: the independent classes σ, σ, and
g ∗ c1 (HC ) restrict to zero, whereas an ample class restricts nontrivially; here HC is ample
and b2 (X) = 4. The inclusion T ,→ C ◦ induces a surjection of fundamental groups, because
loops in a smooth complex variety can be moved off a closed subset of positive complex
codimension. Since V |T is the restriction of the same local system, its monodromy image is
unchanged. Therefore
                                     dimR (Λ2 VR,t )π1 (T,t) = 1.                       (10.16)
Invariants commute with extension from Q to R: they are the common kernels of rational
monodromy-minus-identity maps, and a finite subcollection suffices in finite dimension.
    If L were nonzero and proper, the polarization composed with the two flat projections
onto L and L⊥ would give two independent real invariant alternating forms. The polarization
identifies the dual alternating-square representation with Λ2 VR , so this contradicts (10.16).
Rationality of L is not needed. If L = VR , the Higgs field vanishes identically, contradicting
the chosen nonzero variation of g (which remains nonzero on every dense open). Thus L = 0.
If r < n, every maximum-rank matrix would have a nonzero kernel, making K all nonzero.
Hence r = n.

Proof of Proposition 10.1. Suppose 0 ̸= α ∈ H a,b (S) with a > b. The flat section z in (10.4)
is fixed by the joint algebraic monodromy G, and its component (10.5) is nonzero. At a point

t ∈ T , choose an invertible matrix A ∈ Et and a lift ν ∈ g−1,1
                                                           t    . Let NV be its induced action
on Λa Vt and NU its action on Utb , acting on their respective tensor factors. Invariance gives

                                        (NV + NU )zt = 0.

Since Utb has Hodge types (p, b − p) with 0 ≤ p ≤ b, the operator NU satisfies NUb+1 = 0. The
two tensor actions commute, so induction gives

                                     NVa zt = (−1)a NUa zt = 0,                           (10.17)

because a > b.
    On Vt , the operator ν maps H1,t isomorphically onto H 1,t , and kills H 1,t . Consequently
  a

with all a exterior factors holomorphic, including its Utb factor, it is

The projection of zt to this sector is nonzero, because its further projection to (Utb )0,b is the
nonzero component (10.5). The displayed map is injective and every other exterior sector is

    It follows that all odd cohomology of S vanishes and

since h0,0 (S) = 1. Rational cohomology of the smooth stack is that of its coarse space,
including when its stabilizers are ineffective. This proves the stated positive Euler character-
istic.


11     Eliminating quotient singularities
Proposition 11.1 (Smoothness under a finite cover by an abelian variety). Let f : X → B
be the original projective Lagrangian fibration in the hypotheses of Theorem 1.1. Suppose that
B has quotient singularities and that there is a finite surjective morphism

                                            p : A −→ B

from an abelian variety A of dimension n. Then B is smooth.
    The two inputs have different roles. The original fibration supplies the direct-image
identity and the Fano property of B. The abelian variety supplies translation fields, and the
Fano property will make the ramification line bundle of the lifted cover ample. No Galois
property of p is assumed. The proof therefore retains all arrows of its relation over the
canonical stack, together with their labels.

11.1    The canonical stack and its labelled relation
Write B for the smooth canonical Deligne–Mumford stack of B, and c : B → B for its
coarse morphism. Its local presentations are [U/G], where U is smooth and the finite group
G acts faithfully and freely in codimension one. These presentations can equivalently be
obtained from the maximal charts of Proposition 3.3: their normalized overlaps are now
étale everywhere. In particular, B is proper, has trivial generic stabilizer, and agrees with B
over Breg . All fiber products of stacks below are 2-fiber products. An arrow in such a fiber
product retains its isomorphism label, even when its two coarse coordinates coincide.

                                                      85
Lemma 11.2 (Representable lifts). The maps f and p lift to representable morphisms

                     fe : X −→ B,          pe : A −→ B,     cfe = f,    cpe = p.

The first is projective and equidimensional of relative dimension n, with connected fibers. The
second is finite, flat, and surjective.
Proof. Take a local Galois chart U → U/G as above. Its non-étale locus on the coarse space
has codimension at least two. The inverse image of that locus has codimension at least two
in X, by equidimensionality, and in A, by finiteness. Consequently, the full normalizations
of the components of the pullbacks of U → U/G that dominate these smooth sources are
finite, by excellence, and étale over the sources, by purity of the branch locus [56, Tag 0BMB].
Their deck actions are torsor actions: the torsor identities hold on the dense étale locus and
extend uniquely to the finite étale covers. The torsors and their equivariant maps to U are
precisely the data defining the two lifts. Uniqueness on normalized overlaps makes these
data compatible and gives the stated representable lifts. The normalized-source package of
Proposition 3.3 gives the asserted properties of fe on each chart: its source is smooth and
symplectic, its map to U is projective with Lagrangian equidimensional fibers, and Stein
factorization gives connected fibers and direct image OU .
    The pullback of pe to U is finite, since it is finite over the ordinary finite base change
A ×B U . It is a morphism between smooth n-dimensional varieties and is flat by miracle
flatness [56, Tag 00R4]. Thus pe is finite flat, and it is surjective because its coarse morphism
is surjective.

    The lifted cover defines the finite relation
                                 t
                            −
                 R = A ×B A −
                            −→
                             →A,              δ : R −→ A,        δ(ξ) = t(ξ) − s(ξ).          (11.1)
                                 s


An arrow with source x and target y is a triple (x, y, η), where η : pe(x) ≃ pe(y) is its isomorphism
label. Both projections are finite flat. The relation is generically étale over A, hence reduced:
a nilpotent section would be zero generically and would contradict torsion-freeness over the
integral base A. It is pure-dimensional of dimension n, and every irreducible component is
finite and surjective over A under both projections. If ν : Z → R is the normalization of a
component followed by inclusion, we also write s, t for the induced coordinate maps on Z,
and put δZ = δ ◦ ν.
    The identity section i : A → R is a closed immersion. The inertia stack I(B) parametrizes
a point of B together with one of its automorphisms. Thus

                                         δ −1 (0) = A ×B I(B).

In this zero-difference fiber the image of i is both open and closed: the identity section of the
finite unramified inertia is open, and separatedness makes it closed. We call its complement
the nonidentity part. In particular, a point in that complement is an arrow (x, x, h) with
h ̸= 1; its label is retained even though its two coordinates agree.
    Retain the ample line bundle H on B chosen in Section 2, and put P = p∗ H on A. The
two coordinate pullbacks of P to R are isomorphic. For an abelian subvariety D ⊂ A, define
its polarized complement by
                                     λ            0
                     D⊥ = ker A −−→
                                  P
                                    Ab −→ D
                                          b ,             λP (a) = t∗a P ⊗ P −1 .             (11.2)

The superscript 0 means identity component. The nondegenerate rational alternating form of
the polarization gives dim D⊥ = n − dim D, (D⊥ )⊥ = D, and reversal of inclusions under
this operation.

                                                  86
    Here is the maximality argument that will eliminate nontrivial stabilizers. If a nontrivial
stabilizer existed, surjectivity of pe would give a nonidentity arrow of difference zero. Choose,
among all relation components containing such an arrow, one with difference image of largest
dimension. We first construct one translation field v on A. For every nonidentity automor-
phism h, the h-fixed projection of dpe(v) will be nonzero at every lift of the corresponding
inertia point. This will force each of these difference images to be proper in A. The equality
of the two polarization pullbacks will then make the chosen image a torsion coset τ + D.
It contains zero, so τ ∈ D and the image is the subgroup D itself. Its properness makes
K = D⊥ positive-dimensional.
    Finally, ramification will supply another relation component with differences outside D.
On the normalization of the chosen component, a K-translation will move a point above
nonidentity inertia over a ramification divisor. A whole divisor through that translated point
will still map onto D. We will place that divisor and its marked point in one component
of the space of composable arrows. Composition on this component preserves the marked
nonidentity arrow and produces a difference image strictly larger than D, contradicting the
choice of dimension. The next calculation constructs the translation field needed for the first
step.

11.2    A translation field along nonidentity inertia
Lemma 11.3 (A K-theory identity). In K 0 (B),
                                           n
                                               (−1)j [ΩjB ] = λ−1 (Ω1B ).
                                           X
                              fe [OX ] =
                               !                                                          (11.3)
                                           j=0

Moreover, for some integer m > 0,
                                                    m
                                   [TB ] − n[OB ]        λ−1 (Ω1B ) = 0.                  (11.4)
                                                           ∼    [j]
Proof. Use the global isomorphisms ϕj : Rj f∗ OX − → ΩB chosen once on the original base in
Section 2, with ϕ0 the unit isomorphism. We apply the chart comparison of Proposition 4.7.
Choose a connected affine quotient chart U → U/G with U/G → B an integral separated étale
neighborhood, and view U → B as its atlas map. Write fU : XU → U for the normalized lift,
put U 0 = U ×B Breg , and let π : U 0 → Breg be the induced étale morphism. The complement
of U 0 has codimension at least two. Over this open the lift is the ordinary base change
of the original fibration. Ordinary flat base change for π, followed by the étale cotangent
identification, gives
                                                                       π ∗ ϕj
                      (Rj (fU )∗ OXU )|U 0 ≃ π ∗ (Rj f∗ OX |Breg ) −−−−→ ΩjU 0 .
This open includes the discriminant over the regular part of B; the flat map used here is π,
so no smoothness of the fibers is required for the base-change step [56, Tag 02KH].
    For j > 0, Ou’s local reflexivity theorem applies to fU : Proposition 3.3 supplies its
projective, equidimensional map with connected fibers and smooth canonical-trivial source,
and U is smooth [46, Theorem 1.3]. Both Rj (fU )∗ OXU and ΩjU are therefore their reflexive
extensions from U 0 . The displayed isomorphism extends uniquely across the complement.
For j = 0 the same conclusion is the unit isomorphism from (fU )∗ OXU = OU . On a chart
overlap the extended maps agree over the inverse image of Breg , by transitivity of flat base
change from the same ϕj . Every component of the overlap meets this open, since it maps
étale to a chart and cannot be supported over its codimension-at-least-two complement.
Uniqueness of reflexive extension makes the maps agree everywhere. Thus they glue to
degreewise isomorphisms
                              Rj fe∗ OX ≃ ΩjB      (0 ≤ j ≤ n).

                                                   87
For j > n, the restriction to U 0 is zero by Proposition 2.1, and reflexivity gives zero on U .
Thus no higher direct-image terms occur.
    These isomorphisms first give (11.3) in coherent K-theory. In this setting coherent and
vector-bundle K-theory agree. Indeed, TB is faithful on every stabilizer by linearization of
finite group actions. A finite direct sum G of bounded tensor powers of this bundle and
its dual contains every irreducible stabilizer representation occurring on a finite system of
quotient charts. Exactness of finite-group invariants makes the evaluation

                                    c∗ c∗ (G ∨ ⊗ F) ⊗ G −→ F

surjective for every coherent F. For sufficiently large k, the coherent sheaf c∗ (G ∨ ⊗ F) ⊗ H k
is globally generated on B. Its generators yield a surjection from a finite sum of G ⊗ c∗ H −k
onto F. Iterating and using regularity of the smooth stack gives a finite locally free resolution.
This proves the claimed comparison and allows the identity to be pulled back in vector-bundle
K 0 , even along an inertia morphism which is not flat.
     We spell out the nilpotence needed for (11.4). If V is a vector bundle of rank r on a
smooth projective variety Y , then [V ] − r is nilpotent in K 0 (Y ). Indeed, pull back to the full
flag bundle of V . This pullback is injective on K 0 , since pushforward of its structure sheaf is
OY , with no higher direct images. The pulled-back class is a sum of classes [L] − 1 for line
bundles. For a globally generated line bundle L, a finite set of generating sections and its
exact Koszul complex show that (1 − [L−1 ])N = 0 for some N . An arbitrary line bundle is a
quotient of two sufficiently positive globally generated line bundles. Therefore its class minus
one is also nilpotent: it is an invertible multiple of a difference of two commuting nilpotent
elements. A finite sum of commuting nilpotents is nilpotent. This proves the assertion.
     Apply it on the original projective X to fe∗ TB . The projection formula and (11.3) give
(11.4), with one exponent m before any inertia component is chosen.

Lemma 11.4 (A translation field with no fixed-direction zero). For each connected component
S of the nonidentity inertia of B, let j : S → B be its natural morphism, let h denote the
tautological finite-order automorphism, and set

       d = dim S,       W = A ×B S,            a : W → A,       q : W → S,     E = q ∗ TS .

There is a translation-invariant vector field v ∈ Lie(A) such that, simultaneously for every
such S, the section

                         sv,S ∈ H 0 (W, E),
                                                                        
                                                   sv,S = prh=1 dpe(v)|W ,                    (11.5)

has no zero.

Proof. An inertia component is a smooth proper stack: on a quotient chart its presentation is
a fixed locus U h modulo the appropriate centralizer. The fixed eigensubbundle of j ∗ TB is TS .
Also, W is a scheme, since it is finite over A. The morphism W → S is finite flat, being a base
change of pe. Thus W is projective, pure-dimensional of dimension d, and Cohen–Macaulay.
These assertions hold even if W is nonreduced.
    Restrict (11.4) to S and take the character of the tautological automorphism. This is the
inertia eigenbundle character used in Toën’s Riemann–Roch framework [57, Section 3.3 and
Lemma 4.6]; only its explicitly described ring homomorphism and moving-factor cancellation
are needed here. More explicitly, for a bundle V on B, decompose j ∗ V = ζ Vζ into the
                                                                               L

h-eigenbundles and use the ring homomorphism
                                          X

                                          ζ


                                                  88
Pull this identity to W and take its Chern character in H even (W, C). The degree-zero term
coming from [TB ] − n is tr(h|TB ) − n, which is nonzero. Indeed, the tangent action is faithful
and all eigenvalues have absolute value one; their sum can be n only if every eigenvalue is one.
This would make h the identity by finite-order linearization. The character of each moving
cotangent factor in the alternating exterior sum has nonzero degree-zero term (1 − ζ)rk Vζ ,
for ζ ̸= 1. The eigenbundle ranks are constant on connected S, so each of these degree-zero

    Consider the bundle map

has dimension at most r + (n − r) = n. There are finitely many rank strata. Thus a general u

                                              89
11.3    Difference images and translations
We now use the field to control every relation component meeting the nonidentity part of
δ −1 (0). The first step rules out a dominant difference image; the polarization will then
describe each remaining image and its translation symmetry.

Lemma 11.5 (Dominant differences avoid nonidentity inertia). If an irreducible component
of R contains a nonidentity arrow with difference zero, its difference image is a proper
subvariety of A.

Proof. Let Z be the normalization of such a component, let z0 ∈ Z map to the given arrow,
and suppose that δZ : Z → A is dominant. Take its Stein factorization
                                             β        γ
                                          Z−
                                           →T −
                                              → A.

Here T is normal, γ is finite and dominant, and β has connected fibers. The connected fiber
through z0 lies entirely over nonidentity arrows: its image lies in δ −1 (0), where the identity
and nonidentity parts are open and closed.
    Put t0 = β(z0 ), and take the field v of Lemma 11.4. A finite dominant map to the smooth
n-fold A is locally open. For completeness, a sufficiently small local branch of T at t0 is finite
over a neighborhood of 0; its analytic image is closed and has dimension n, so contains a
neighborhood of 0. We can therefore lift a sequence exp(εν v), with 0 ̸= εν → 0, to points of
T tending to t0 . Lift these points farther to Z. Properness gives a subsequence tending to a
point z∞ ∈ β −1 (t0 ). Its arrow is nonidentity, and the arrows in the sequence have coordinates

                     (xν , yν ),   yν = xν + exp(εν v),        xν , yν −→ x∞ .

    Choose a local quotient chart at pe(x∞ ), a lift ψ of pe on a small analytic neighborhood of
x∞ , and finite-group linearizing coordinates at ψ(x∞ ). After passing to a subsequence the
labels are one fixed group element h ̸= 1, fixing ψ(x∞ ), and

                                        ψ(yν ) = hψ(xν ).

Projecting to the h-fixed coordinate space yields
                                                                
                              prh=1 ψ(xν + exp(εν v)) − ψ(xν ) = 0.

Division by εν and passage to the limit give prh=1 dψx∞ (v) = 0. This is a zero of the section
(11.5) on the inertia component containing h, contrary to Lemma 11.4.

Lemma 11.6 (Difference images and complementary translations). Let Z be the normalization
of an irreducible component of R, and suppose that its difference image DZ has dimension
r < n. Then
                                      DZ = τ + D
for an abelian subvariety D ⊂ A of dimension r and a torsion point τ . If K = D⊥ , the image
of Z in A × A is stable under diagonal translation by K. This action lifts uniquely to Z; both
coordinate morphisms are equivariant, and δZ is invariant.

Proof. Let UZ ⊂ DZ be a nonempty open over which every irreducible fiber component of δZ
has dimension n − r, let a ∈ UZ , and let F be any one of those components, with its reduced
structure. On F the second coordinate map is ta composed with the first. The equality of
the two pulled-back polarizations therefore says

                                       (s|F )∗ λP (a) ≃ OF .                               (11.7)

                                                 90
Let HF ⊂ A be the connected abelian subvariety generated by differences of points of s(F ).
Since s is finite,
                                   dim HF ≥ n − r.
Resolve F and choose a base point on its resolution. Its map to A factors through a surjective
homomorphism from its Albanese variety onto HF , followed by a translation. The dual
homomorphism H     b F → Pic0 (Fe ) has finite kernel. Equation (11.7) thus implies that the
restriction of λP (a) to HF is torsion.
    For a fixed abelian subvariety H ⊂ A, the set of points with this property is a countable
union of torsion translates of H ⊥ . To check the assertion including disconnected kernels, the
homomorphism
                                  A −→ H, b      a 7−→ λP (a)|H
is surjective and has H ⊥ as the identity component of its kernel; the induced map A/H ⊥ → H    b
is an isogeny. The inverse image of any torsion point is consequently a finite union of cosets of
H ⊥ represented by torsion points of A. Here torsion points lift through a quotient of abelian
varieties; this follows, for example, by choosing an isogeny complement to the kernel.
    There are only countably many abelian subvarieties of A, since their rational first-homology
subspaces determine them. There are also only countably many torsion points. The open UZ
is therefore covered by countably many closed torsion cosets, each of dimension at most r. A
complex irreducible variety cannot be covered by countably many proper closed subvarieties:
apply the Baire theorem in a small analytic ball in its smooth locus, and then use irreducibility.
Hence one such coset contains DZ . The dimension bound forces equality, proving DZ = τ + D
with dim D = r.
    This argument also determines the subgroup generated by every component over a dense
set of very general points. Let a range over UZ minus every coset in the preceding countable
collection which does not contain DZ . This complement is dense by the same Baire argument.
For each fiber component F over each such a, its character constraint supplies a coset of HF⊥
of dimension at most r containing a. That coset must contain DZ and hence equal it. Thus

                                HF⊥ = D,        HF = D⊥ = K.

The image s(F ) lies in one K-coset and has dimension n − r = dim K, so it is the whole
coset. Consequently the relation over every such very general a is stable under diagonal
K-translation. These fibers are dense in Z: every nonempty Zariski open in Z has image
containing a nonempty Zariski open in DZ , which meets this set. Taking closures therefore
proves this invariance on the full image Γ ⊂ A × A.
    The finite map from the original relation component to Γ is generically one-to-one: over
Breg there is a unique isomorphism label for a pair of points. Each component dominates A,
so this open is dense on it. Thus Z is also the normalization of Γ. The morphism K × Z → Γ
induced by simultaneous translation is dominant and has normal source. It therefore lifts
uniquely to the normalization Z. The action law, equivariance of the coordinate maps, and
invariance of the difference follow by uniqueness of this lift, or by checking on the dense
normal isomorphism locus and extending.

11.4    Ramification and maximality
We now begin the contradiction proving Proposition 11.1. Suppose that B has a nontrivial
stabilizer. Surjectivity of pe gives a nonidentity point of δ −1 (0). Among the irreducible
components of R containing such a point, choose one, denoted R0 , whose difference image
has largest dimension r. Write
                                      ν : Z −→ R0 ,→ R


                                               91
for its normalization followed by inclusion. By Lemma 11.5, r < n. Lemma 11.6 makes δZ (Z)
a torsion coset; since it contains zero, it is an abelian subvariety D ⊊ A. Put K = D⊥ , and
fix one point z∗ ∈ Z above a nonidentity zero-difference arrow.
    The next two lemmas provide exactly the data needed for one composition. The first
gives a ramification divisor L and an actual residual component containing all of i(L), with a
difference outside D. The second moves z∗ over L and places the translated point on a whole
divisor whose difference image is D.

Lemma 11.7 (Ramification and a residual relation). Let D ⊊ A be an abelian subvariety
and K = D⊥ . There is a prime component L of the ramification divisor of pe : A → B which
maps surjectively to A/K. For such an L, there is one irreducible component R′ ̸= i(A) of
the actual relation R such that

                                      i(L) ⊂ R′ ,            δ(R′ ) ̸⊂ D.

Proof. Since pe is generically étale, the determinant of dpe defines an effective Cartier ramifica-
tion divisor Ram(pe) on A, and
                                                             −1
                                         OA (Ram(pe)) ≃ pe∗ KB  .

There is no divisorial stabilizer on B. A sufficiently divisible power of its canonical bundle is
therefore the pullback of OB (mKB ). Thus
                                          −1 ⊗m
                                    (pe∗ KB )   ≃ p∗ OB (−mKB ).

The right side is ample because p is finite and −KB is ample. Hence the line bundle of the
total ramification divisor is ample.
    Since D ̸= A, the group K is positive-dimensional. If every prime component of Ram(pe)
had proper image in A/K, a general K-coset would miss the entire divisor. Its defining
section would trivialize the restricted ample line bundle on that positive-dimensional projective
coset, a contradiction. Thus one component L dominates A/K; properness makes this map
surjective. This uses ampleness of the total ramification divisor and does not assert that each
of its components is ample.
    The generic point of L lies over Breg , because finiteness preserves the codimension-at-
least-two bound for the inverse image of Bsing . Near a general point of L, the map between
smooth spaces has the transverse form

                        (u1 , u2 , . . . , un ) 7−→ (ue1 , u2 , . . . , un ),   e > 1.

Its self fiber product has, besides the identity branch, branches v1 = ζu1 , vj = uj for
ζ e = 1, ζ ̸= 1. They meet the identity along L. There are only finitely many global
irreducible components of R. Their closed intersections with i(L), excluding the identity
component, contain a dense open of i(L). Irreducibility therefore gives a single residual
component R′ ̸= i(A) containing all of i(L), including its special points.
    Suppose, for a contradiction, that δ(R′ ) ⊂ D. Lemma 11.6 says that this difference image
is a torsion coset. It contains zero, since i(L) ⊂ R′ , so it is an abelian subvariety D′ ⊂ D. Its
polarized complement K ′ = (D′ )⊥ contains K. The same lemma gives a K-action on the
normalization Z ′ of R′ .
    Consider the closed set
                                    F = {x ∈ A : i(x) ∈ R′ }.
It is proper: otherwise R′ would contain i(A), and the two irreducible n-dimensional com-
ponents would be equal. For any x ∈ F , choose an individual preimage of i(x) in Z ′ . Its

                                                        92
connected K-orbit remains over difference zero and remains in the inverse image of the
identity part of δ −1 (0), since that part is open and closed. Its image at k is therefore precisely
i(x + k). Hence F is K-invariant. This reasoning does not require a global lift of i(L) to Z ′ .
    The divisor L ⊂ F is an irreducible component of F , because F is proper in the n-fold A.
A connected group preserves each irreducible component of a closed invariant set with finitely
many components. Thus K + L = L. But L → A/K is surjective, which gives K + L = A.
This contradiction proves the required assertion about δ(R′ ).

Lemma 11.8 (A divisor through the marked arrow). Let ν : Z → R be the normalization of
an irreducible component followed by inclusion, with difference image an abelian subvariety
D ⊊ A, and put K = D⊥ . Suppose z0 ∈ Z lies over a nonidentity arrow of difference zero.
For L as in Lemma 11.7, there are k ∈ K, e0 = k · z0 , and a reduced prime divisor E ⊂ Z
through e0 , such that x0 := s(e0 ) = t(e0 ) ∈ L, ν(e0 ) is nonidentity, and

                                   s(E) = L,         δZ (E) = D.

Proof. The action of K supplied by Lemma 11.6 preserves difference zero. Connectedness and
the open-and-closed identity partition preserve the nonidentity condition. Since L → A/K is
surjective, a translate of the first coordinate of z0 belongs to L. Choose such a k, and put
e0 = k · z0 and x0 = s(e0 ) = t(e0 ). This is the translated normalization point retained below.
    The divisor L is Cartier because A is smooth. Its local equation pulls back to a nonzero
nonunit in the integral local ring OZ,e0 . A minimal prime over that equation has height one,
by the principal ideal theorem. Taking its closure with reduced structure gives a prime divisor
E through e0 and contained in s−1 (L). The finite map s : Z → A preserves dimension, so its
closed image s(E) has dimension n − 1 and equals the irreducible divisor L.
    The image of the proper action morphism K × E → Z is closed and irreducible. Its
first-coordinate image is K + L = A. Since Z → A is finite, this image has dimension n and
hence is all of Z. As δZ is K-invariant,

                               δZ (E) = δZ (K · E) = δZ (Z) = D.

These are equalities of actual images; projectivity ensures that no special point is lost by
taking closures.

Completion of the proof of Proposition 11.1. Apply Lemmas 11.7 and 11.8 to the fixed
D, K, Z, z∗ . They provide a divisor L ⊂ A, a residual component R′ ⊂ R containing all of
i(L), and a reduced prime divisor E ⊂ Z through the translated point e0 , with

                                  δZ (E) = D,         δ(R′ ) ̸⊂ D.

Write x0 = s(e0 ) = t(e0 ) ∈ L and α0 = ν(e0 ). The arrow α0 is nonidentity and has difference
zero. It is this translated arrow that composition will retain. Form the space of composable
arrows
                                       T = R′ × t,A,s Z.
The inclusion of all of i(L) in the actual, possibly nonnormal component R′ gives a global
morphism i|L : L → R′ : both L and R′ carry their reduced structures. Hence the map
                                                                     
                               ι : E −→ T ,       e 7−→ i(s(e)), e

is a closed embedding, the graph of this morphism over the closed subscheme E ⊂ Z. Since
E is irreducible, one irreducible component T0 contains its entire image. Give T0 its reduced
structure. Since E is reduced, ι factors as a closed embedding into T0 ; in particular, this

                         T0 is one irreducible component of R′ ×t,A,s Z

Figure 2: The component used for composition. The whole divisor E, including e0 , lies in

the subsequent composition.
    This same component dominates both factors. Indeed, R′ × Z has pure dimension 2n.
The equations for T are the pullback of the diagonal in A × A, which is locally cut out by n
equations. The dimension inequality for an ideal with n generators implies dim T0 ≥ n. Both
projections from T to its factors are finite, so dim T0 ≤ n. Its images in both irreducible n-
dimensional factors are closed of dimension n, and thus are the whole factors. The dimension
estimate uses smoothness of A through the equations of its diagonal.
    Composition of the two labelled arrows gives µ : T0 → R. Both T0 and R are finite over
the outer source coordinate in A, so µ is finite. Its closed irreducible image has dimension n,
since that source coordinate is surjective. Consequently its image is an irreducible component
R′′ of R. On the embedded E, composition with the identity is exactly ν(e), with its label.
Thus R′′ contains the marked nonidentity arrow α0 = ν(e0 ) over difference zero, and its
difference image contains all of δZ (E) = D.
    It also contains a point outside D. In fact, dominance of T0 → R′ allows us to choose
composable arrows
                                         z −→ x −→ y
with x − z ∈/ D, whereas y − x ∈ D, because the second arrow comes from Z. Since D is
a subgroup, y − z = (y − x) + (x − z) ∈  / D. The closed irreducible difference image of R′′
therefore contains D properly, and has dimension greater than r. It still meets nonidentity
inertia over zero, contradicting the choice of r.
    Thus every stabilizer is trivial. A Deligne–Mumford stack with trivial inertia is an
algebraic space, and its coarse morphism is then an isomorphism. Therefore B = B, and B
is smooth.




                                                    94
12     Completion of the proof
Proof of Theorem 1.1. The case n = 1 was settled in Section 2; assume n > 1. By Lemma 2.3,
b2 (X) ≥ 4. If b2 (X) ≥ 5, Proposition 7.6 gives quotient singularities for B. If b2 (X) = 4
and the polarized family of smooth fibers of the original f is isotrivial, Proposition 8.1
gives the same conclusion. In the remaining case, Proposition 9.6 chooses the two systems
of Proposition 9.1 on a projective IHS deformation X ′ , still with b2 (X ′ ) = 4: projective
Lagrangian fibrations f ′ : X ′ → B and g : X ′ → C, with C normal and projective, such that
g has nonzero polarized variation and f ′ restricts to a finite surjection on every smooth fiber
of g. If B had a nonquotient germ, a maximal chart would be singular. Propositions 4.3
and 4.5 would supply the fixed stratum (S0 , S) and its product presentations. Proposition 6.1,
applied to the original fibration, would give χtop (S0 ) = 0. For the two fibrations on X ′ ,
Proposition 10.1 instead gives χtop (S0 ) > 0, a contradiction. Thus every germ of B is a
quotient singularity in this case as well. The three cases exhaust the possibilities.
    Using the fixed-base family, Proposition 9.1 supplies in every case a finite cover of the
unchanged B by an n-dimensional abelian variety: restrict its first fibration to a smooth fiber
of the second. This cover, the quotient singularities just proved, and the original projective
fibration f : X → B meet the hypotheses of Proposition 11.1. Hence B is smooth. The
original X is projective, f is surjective with connected positive-dimensional fibers, and B is
now smooth and projective. Hwang’s theorem applies with precisely these hypotheses [26,
Theorem 1.2, in the setting of Theorem 1.1], giving B ≃ PnC .
